- Block 1 (12): 4 rounds — A1 Squat level choice 8 to 10 reps / A2 Row level choice 10 to 12 reps / A3 Dead bug 8 each. Rest 60s.
- Block 2 (12): 3 rounds — B1 Hinge level choice 10 / B2 Push level choice 8 to 12 / B3 Carry 30m.
- Finisher (8): 4 × (40s work / 20s rest) rotating: bike, wall ball (level), sled push, ski.
- Cool down (3).
Each exercise slot lists G1 to G4 options (use the grade ladder at the top of each File 03 category).

### 13.4 Golf programme (2 to 3 days/week)
Principles: hip rotation (IR/ER) and T spine mobility, rotational power from the ground up (Gambetta), anti rotation core, single leg stability (lead leg), grip, glute strength, shoulder health, and avoiding loaded spinal twisting with flexion.
- Warm up: 90/90 hip switches, open book, T spine rotations in lunge, hip CARs, band pull aparts.
- Power: med ball rotational scoop toss and shot put throw (3×5 each side), lateral bounds (Teams only, G3+, screened; never in groups).
- Strength: trap bar/KB deadlift, RFESS, single leg RDL, landmine press, TRX/chest supported row, Pallof press, Copenhagen side plank, cable woodchop (controlled), farmers carry.
- Mobility finish: hip IR stretch, thread the needle, lat stretch.
- Off season: build strength (Block 2/3 loading). In season: maintain 1 to 2 shorter sessions, keep power, reduce soreness inducing eccentric volume before rounds.

### 13.5 Seniors programme (65+ or low capacity)
Principles: balance and gait every session, floor to stand practice, strength still progressive (just starting lower), power with safe tools (fast concentric on sit to stand, med ball throws, sled), carries, confidence.
- Warm up: seated/standing march, arm circles, sit to stand ×10, heel to toe walk, side steps, calf raises with support.
- Strength: sit to stand (box height regression), TRX assisted squat, step ups (low), wall/incline push ups, TRX/cable rows, KB deadlift from blocks, glute bridges, supported split squat.
- Power: fast sit to stand (stand up as fast as possible, lower slowly), med ball chest pass, sled push.
- Balance: tandem stance, single leg holds near support, stepping drills, clock reaches.
- Floor transfers: half kneeling to stand, get up progressions.
- Carries: farmers carry light to moderate.
- Conditioning: bike, rower steady intervals.

### 13.6 Fat loss emphasis (Cosgrove hierarchy)
Nutrition first (deficit, protein ~1.6 to 2.2 g/kg target bodyweight, coaching), 3 strength sessions (keep loads heavy enough to maintain muscle), 2 to 3 metabolic finishers/intervals, daily steps. Don't turn every session into cardio.

### 13.7 Muscle / strength emphasis
Higher weekly set volume (12 to 16 per muscle), accumulation/intensification blocks, double progression, longer rests on main lifts, sufficient protein and calories.

---

## SECTION 14. PROGRAMME OUTPUT FORMAT (for the AI)

Every programme the AI writes should use this structure so coaches can read it fast and the app can parse it:

```
MEMBER: [name] | PROGRAMME: [Teams/Group/Golf/Seniors] | BLOCK: [1/2/3] WEEK: [1-4] | DAY: [A/B/C]
GOAL: [...] | FLAGS: [injuries / considerations] | READINESS RULE: traffic light

WARM UP (10 min)
- [drill] [reps/time]

BLOCK A (Core/Power)
A1 [exercise] | [sets] x [reps] | tempo [x-x-x-x] | rest [s] | RPE [x] | Progress: [model] | Regress: [option] | Cue: [one key cue]
A2 ...

BLOCK B (Strength)
B1 ...
B2 ...

BLOCK C (Accessory)
C1 ...

FINISHER
[protocol] | [level targets]

COOL DOWN
[...]

COACH NOTES: [what to watch, what to progress next session]
```

Rules: give one primary cue per exercise (members can't use five); include at least one regression for every exercise; keep exercise names exactly as in File 03 so the app can match them.

---

## SECTION 15. COACHING LANGUAGE AND CUEING PRINCIPLES

- One cue at a time. External cues (focus on the environment) often work better than internal ones: "push the floor away" beats "contract your quads".
- Teach in this order: position → breath/brace → movement → speed.
- Praise specific things. Correct one thing per set.
- Explain the why in one sentence ("this keeps your knees healthy for skiing next year"). Members who understand why adhere better.
- Celebrate PBs, level ups and retest improvements publicly (with permission). This builds the environment Optimal sells.


============================================================
PART 3. EXERCISE LIBRARY
============================================================

3A. HOW TO READ THE LIBRARY
- Grades: G1 Foundation (supported, assisted, lowest load). G2 Standard. G3 Advanced (more load, single arm/leg, paused, unstable). G4 Maximum (heavy barbell, advanced single leg, high skill; mostly Teams).
- Move up a grade after 2 sessions completing all reps at RPE 7 or less, pain free, technique signed off. Move down on amber/red readiness days or pain above 3/10.
- Each exercise line: ID | Name | Grade | Family (look up 3B for why, cues, faults, cautions, levers, prescription) | Kit | Sessions (B=BURN, S=STRONG, H=HYBRID) | Group Y/N | Impact (only shown if not none) | flags | Up (named progressions) | Down (named regressions) | Swap (same level) | Watch (cautions) | Tags (variation notes, see 3D).
- Group N means not for group sessions (impact or heavy/high skill); use the family's group swap or a G3 regression.
- Flags: T = Teams only, S = suitable for seniors. Kit marked * is not on the confirmed equipment list; Optimal's equipment rules decide.
- Use exercise names exactly as written so the app can match them.

3B. EXERCISE FAMILIES (full coaching detail, written once)

FAMILY agility | Agility / locomotion
Muscles: Whole lower body, coordination, conditioning
Why: Change of direction, rhythm and footwork. Builds coordination and reaction (Gambetta), useful for sport and falls prevention, but most variations involve running or hopping.
Cues: 1) Stay low and light on the feet 2) Short quick steps, eyes up 3) Control each change of direction
Faults: Crossing feet on shuffles; Standing tall and slow; Doing too much volume
Cautions: IMPACT (running/hopping): excluded from group sessions. Teams only after screening.
Progress by: Faster, more complex patterns
Regress by: Walking footwork patterns, lateral band walk, sled lateral drag
Default prescription: 4 to 8 x 10 to 20s, full recovery | Sessions: HYBRID
Sources: Gambetta

FAMILY tricep | Arms: triceps extension
Muscles: Triceps
Why: Triceps drive all pressing and elbow strength. Overhead extensions stretch the long head for more muscle growth.
Cues: 1) Elbows pointing up/forward and fixed 2) Lower behind the head with control 3) Extend fully, squeeze
Faults: Elbows flaring; Arching the back; Using momentum
Cautions: Elbow pain: use a rope or band, lighter.
Progress by: Heavier, slower
Regress by: Band, lighter
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Poliquin

FAMILY balance | Balance and proprioception
Muscles: Foot, ankle, hip stabilisers, nervous system
Why: Balance falls away quickly with age and inactivity; falls are a leading cause of injury in older adults. Single leg balance is part of the Optimal screen. Train it every session for 60+ (Gambetta: postural alignment and dynamic balance are the foundation).
Cues: 1) Stand tall near a support 2) Grip the floor with the foot, soft knee 3) Hold or reach while keeping the hips level
Faults: Locking the knee; Holding the breath; Doing it far from support
Cautions: Always near a rail or rig for seniors.
Progress by: Eyes closed, cushion, reach, catch a ball
Regress by: Hand on support, tandem stance
Default prescription: 3 x 20 to 30s each leg | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Gambetta, Falls prevention research

FAMILY sled | Carry / gait: sled push, pull and drag
Muscles: Quads, glutes, calves, whole body conditioning
Why: The sled is the most joint friendly strength and conditioning tool in the gym: concentric only, so almost no soreness, no impact and no technique to fail. Ben Patrick uses backward drags for knee health; DeFranco uses pushes for conditioning. Every member at every level can use it.
Cues: 1) Push: arms extended or bent, body at 45 degrees, drive through the balls of the feet 2) Drag backward: lean back, small steps, push through the front of the foot (knees over toes) 3) Keep steady steps and a neutral spine
Faults: Hips too high or too low; Rounding the back; Taking huge steps that slow the sled
Cautions: Almost universally safe. Backward drag is a knee pain staple. Heavy pushes raise blood pressure: keep breathing.
Progress by: More weight, faster (sprints), longer distance, less rest
Regress by: Lighter, slower, shorter distance
Default prescription: 6 to 10 x 15 to 25m, walk back recovery; or 3 to 5 min backward drag in warm up | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID, STRONG
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG), DeFranco, Boyle

FAMILY mixed_carry | Carry: complex / medley
Muscles: Whole body, grip, core
Why: Combining positions, directions and obstacles adds variety and real world challenge.
Cues: 1) Change positions with control 2) Stand tall through every position 3) Short controlled steps
Faults: Rushing transitions; Losing posture; Too heavy
Cautions: Generally safe.
Progress by: Heavier, longer
Regress by: Lighter, single position
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 40m (or 30 to 45s), rest 60 to 90s | Sessions: HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Dan John, Gambetta

FAMILY farmer | Carry: farmer's carry
Muscles: Grip, traps, core, glutes, posture
Why: Dan John says loaded carries change lives. Picking up heavy things and walking trains grip (a strong longevity predictor), posture, core and conditioning all at once, with low technical demand and low injury risk. Every member, every week.
Cues: 1) Deadlift the weights up properly 2) Stand tall, shoulders down, walk with short controlled steps 3) Breathe and keep the ribs down
Faults: Shrugging; Leaning forward or side to side; Fast sloppy steps
Cautions: Very safe. Pick up and put down with a good hinge. Grip limited members: straps are fine to train the rest.
Progress by: Heavier, longer, on the toes, with steps/obstacles; Dan John standard: bodyweight total for men
Regress by: Lighter, shorter, static hold
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 40m (or 30 to 45s), rest 60 to 90s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Dan John, Attia (grip), Cosgrove

FAMILY rack_carry | Carry: front loaded
Muscles: Core (anti extension), upper back, legs
Why: Holding the weight in front forces the core to resist being pulled forward and the upper back to stay upright. Great posture and core work.
Cues: 1) Weight tight to the chest 2) Stand tall, ribs down, walk slowly 3) Breathe behind the brace
Faults: Leaning back to counterbalance; Holding the breath; Elbows drifting
Cautions: Generally safe. Duck walks are deep knee bending: knee pain caution.
Progress by: Heavier, double KB, uneven
Regress by: Lighter, goblet
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 40m (or 30 to 45s), rest 60 to 90s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Dan John, Cosgrove

FAMILY grip | Carry: grip strength
Muscles: Forearms, hands, shoulders
Why: Grip strength is one of the strongest predictors of healthy ageing and all cause mortality (Attia). Easy to train at the end of sessions.
Cues: 1) Crush the handle/plate 2) Shoulders down and back 3) Hold for time
Faults: Shrugging; Holding the breath; Bent wrists
Cautions: Elbow pain: reduce load.
Progress by: Heavier, thicker grip, longer
Regress by: Lighter, shorter
Default prescription: 3 x 20 to 45s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Attia, Dan John

FAMILY overhead_carry | Carry: overhead / waiter
Muscles: Shoulder stability, rotator cuff, core
Why: Holding weight overhead while walking trains shoulder stability and core in one. Bottoms up holds make the rotator cuff work hard with light weight: brilliant for shoulder rehab.
Cues: 1) Arm locked, biceps by the ear, shoulder packed 2) Ribs down, walk slowly 3) Keep the weight stacked over the shoulder
Faults: Arching the back; Arm drifting forward; Going too heavy
Cautions: Needs pain free overhead range. Waiter's carry (arm at 90 degrees) is an easier option.
Progress by: Heavier, double, bottoms up
Regress by: Waiter's carry, lighter, static hold
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 40m (or 30 to 45s), rest 60 to 90s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Dan John, Cressey

FAMILY suitcase | Carry: suitcase / offset
Muscles: Obliques, QL, grip, glute med
Why: One side loading turns a carry into an anti lateral flexion core exercise: you must stop the body leaning. McGill calls the suitcase carry one of the best core exercises.
Cues: 1) Weight in one hand, stand tall, shoulders level 2) Walk slowly without leaning towards or away from the weight 3) Swap hands
Faults: Leaning; Shrugging the loaded side; Rushing
Cautions: Back pain friendly.
Progress by: Heavier, march with high knees, longer
Regress by: Lighter, static hold
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 40m (or 30 to 45s), rest 60 to 90s each side | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: McGill, Dan John

FAMILY battle_rope | Conditioning: battle ropes
Muscles: Shoulders, arms, core, cardio
Why: Upper body conditioning with no impact. Great in BURN circuits and for members who can't run or jump.
Cues: 1) Athletic stance, brace 2) Drive the waves from the shoulders and hips 3) Keep a steady rhythm
Faults: Standing upright and stiff; Shrugging; Waves dying out
Cautions: Low impact. Rope jumping jacks involve jumping: swap to step jacks in groups.
Progress by: Longer, faster, heavier rope
Regress by: Shorter intervals, kneeling
Default prescription: 6 to 10 x 20 to 30s / 30 to 60s rest | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: Cosgrove (metabolic)

FAMILY erg | Conditioning: erg / walking
Muscles: Heart and lungs, legs (bike/rower), upper body (ski/rower)
Why: The bike, rower and ski erg give big conditioning with no impact, and every level can work at their own intensity. Zone 2 builds the aerobic base (Attia); short sprints suit menopause and fat loss (Sims, Cosgrove hierarchy). Backward walking (treadmill or sled) is Ben Patrick's knee warm up.
Cues: 1) Bike: push and pull with arms and legs, steady cadence 2) Rower: legs, body, arms; then arms, body, legs 3) Ski: hinge and pull down, drive the hips
Faults: Rowing with the arms first; Hunched posture; Going out too hard in intervals
Cautions: Low impact. Rower and ski: watch low back flexion. Treadmill sprints involve impact.
Progress by: Longer work, shorter rest, higher watts/calories, more rounds
Regress by: Easier pace, shorter intervals, more rest
Default prescription: Intervals: e.g. 6 to 10 x 15 to 30s hard / 60 to 90s easy, or 20 to 40 min steady (zone 2) | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: Attia (zone 2, VO2), Cosgrove (intervals), Stacy Sims (sprints), Ben Patrick (backward walking)

FAMILY jacks | Conditioning: jumping cardio
Muscles: Calves, shoulders, heart and lungs
Why: Simple jumping cardio used in warm ups and finishers.
Cues: 1) Light on the toes 2) Rhythm 3) Soft knees
Faults: Heavy landing; Too long; Poor posture
Cautions: IMPACT: excluded from group sessions and avoid with pelvic floor symptoms.
Progress by: Faster, longer
Regress by: Step jacks
Group swap: Group swap: step jacks, marching, assault bike
Default prescription: 30 to 60s | Sessions: BURN
Sources: General conditioning

FAMILY protocol | Conditioning: protocol / format
Muscles: Depends on the exercises chosen
Why: Session formats rather than single exercises. Complexes (Cosgrove) chain lifts without putting the weight down; EMOMs, AMRAPs and ladders control density and keep groups together. Build them only from exercises the group can perform safely while tired.
Cues: 1) Pick 3 to 6 exercises the members already own 2) Set the work:rest and level targets (G1 to G4) 3) Keep quality high, cap volume
Faults: Choosing technical lifts for high fatigue formats; No level options; Too long
Cautions: Exclude jumping in group formats. Avoid heavy spinal loading late in fatigue.
Progress by: More rounds, heavier, less rest
Regress by: Fewer rounds, lighter, more rest
Default prescription: 8 to 20 min blocks | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: Cosgrove (complexes, density), Dan John

FAMILY treadmill_sprint | Conditioning: running based
Muscles: Legs, heart and lungs
Why: Running based conditioning. Effective but impact based.
Cues: 1) Tall posture 2) Quick light steps 3) Arms drive
Faults: Overstriding; Heavy landing; Too much volume
Cautions: IMPACT: excluded from group sessions. Swap to bike/ski/rower/sled.
Progress by: Faster, longer
Regress by: Walk, incline walk, bike
Group swap: Group swap: assault bike, ski erg, rower, sled push, marching high knees
Default prescription: Intervals: e.g. 6 to 10 x 15 to 30s hard / 60 to 90s easy, or 20 to 40 min steady (zone 2) | Sessions: HYBRID
Sources: Gambetta

FAMILY shadow_boxing | Conditioning: shadow boxing
Muscles: Shoulders, core, cardio
Why: Low impact, fun, rotational conditioning.
Cues: 1) Guard up, rotate through the hips 2) Snap punches out and back 3) Stay light on the feet
Faults: Locking elbows; Arms only; Dropping the guard
Cautions: Low impact.
Progress by: Faster, light DBs (no lockout)
Regress by: Slower
Default prescription: 3 to 6 x 30 to 60s | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: General conditioning

FAMILY burpee | Conditioning: total body (burpee family)
Muscles: Whole body
Why: Ground to standing conditioning drills. Huge metabolic demand, but the jump and drop to the floor add impact and wrist/shoulder stress.
Cues: 1) Hands down, step or jump back to plank 2) Chest down (if required), back to feet 3) Stand tall (jump only if allowed)
Faults: Sagging plank; Crashing to the floor; Sloppy as fatigue builds
Cautions: IMPACT when jumping: group sessions use the no jump version (step back, step in, stand). Devil's press and man makers: DB hinge skill required.
Progress by: Add a press, push up or jump
Regress by: Step back burpee, incline burpee onto a bench
Group swap: Group swap: step back burpee (no jump), squat thrust to a bench, wall ball
Default prescription: 3 to 6 x 20 to 40s | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: Cosgrove (metabolic)

FAMILY mountain_climber | Core / conditioning: mountain climbers
Muscles: Core, hip flexors, shoulders, cardio
Why: A plank with running legs. Good conditioning without jumping when done slowly; fast versions add impact through the wrists and shoulders.
Cues: 1) Strong plank, hands under shoulders 2) Drive one knee towards the chest at a time 3) Keep the hips level
Faults: Hips bouncing high; Shoulders drifting behind the hands; Toe tapping without the knee driving
Cautions: Wrist load. Slow and controlled versions are fine for groups; sprint versions are harder.
Progress by: Faster, sliders, cross body
Regress by: Hands on a bench, slow
Default prescription: 3 to 6 x 20 to 30s | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: DeFranco (Limber 11, slow), Cosgrove

FAMILY mcgill_curl | Core: McGill curl up
Muscles: Rectus abdominis (without spinal flexion)
Why: Part of the McGill Big 3: trains the front abs while keeping the low back in neutral. Safer than sit ups and crunches for anyone with back history.
Cues: 1) Lie on your back, one knee bent, hands under the low back 2) Brace and lift the head and shoulders slightly as one unit 3) Hold 8 to 10s, lower
Faults: Tucking the chin; Flattening the low back; Curling up too high
Cautions: Back pain friendly. Part of the Big 3.
Progress by: Raise elbows off the floor, longer holds
Regress by: Shorter holds
Default prescription: Descending pyramid: 6, 4, 2 reps with 8 to 10s holds | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Squat University

FAMILY dead_bug | Core: anti extension (dead bug)
Muscles: Deep abs, hip flexors, coordination
Why: Keeps the low back flat while the arms and legs move: anti extension control and coordination. Safe for almost everyone and the foundation of bracing (McGill, Chek).
Cues: 1) Back flat, ribs down, knees and arms up 2) Slowly extend the opposite arm and leg while breathing out 3) Return and swap without the back arching
Faults: Low back lifting; Moving fast; Holding the breath
Cautions: Back pain friendly. Pelvic floor friendly.
Progress by: Straighter legs, band resisted, KB pullover, slower
Regress by: Heel taps, arms only
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Paul Chek, Boyle

FAMILY hollow | Core: anti extension (hollow body)
Muscles: Abs, hip flexors
Why: A gymnastic anti extension hold. Harder than a dead bug.
Cues: 1) Low back pressed down 2) Arms and legs extended low 3) Hold
Faults: Back arching; Holding breath; Neck strain
Cautions: Can strain the low back if form is lost. Tuck the knees to regress.
Progress by: Longer levers, rocks
Regress by: Tuck hold, dead bug
Default prescription: 3 x 15 to 30s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Gymnastics, Boyle

FAMILY plank | Core: anti extension (plank)
Muscles: Abs, shoulders, glutes
Why: The plank trains the core to resist extension. Useful foundation, part of the Optimal screen (30s kneeling → full plank → plank with full tension). Long lever and RKC versions dramatically increase difficulty without longer holds.
Cues: 1) Elbows under shoulders, straight line head to heels 2) Squeeze glutes, pull the ribs down, push the floor away 3) Breathe, hold with full tension
Faults: Sagging hips; Bum in the air; Holding for minutes with poor form
Cautions: Shoulder or wrist pain: dead bug or Pallof press instead. Prefer 20 to 30s high tension holds over long sloppy ones.
Progress by: Long lever, RKC plank, leg lift, body saw
Regress by: Plank on knees, incline plank on a bench
Default prescription: 3 sets x 20 to 40s (high tension) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: McGill, Boyle, Cosgrove

FAMILY rollout | Core: anti extension (rollout)
Muscles: Abs, lats, shoulders
Why: Rollouts and body saws are advanced anti extension: the further the arms reach, the harder the core works to stop the back arching.
Cues: 1) Kneeling or plank, ribs down, glutes tight 2) Roll or slide out only as far as you can keep the back flat 3) Pull back using the abs and lats
Faults: Low back sagging; Hips piking first; Going too far too soon
Cautions: Can strain the low back if overreached. Shoulder pain: limit range.
Progress by: Longer range, from feet
Regress by: Swiss ball rollout short range, plank
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Boyle, Cosgrove

FAMILY stir | Core: anti extension + anti rotation (Swiss ball)
Muscles: Abs, obliques, shoulders
Why: McGill's favourite advanced core drill: forearms on a Swiss ball making small circles while the body stays rigid.
Cues: 1) Forearms on the ball, plank position 2) Draw small circles with the elbows 3) Keep the hips and back still
Faults: Hips sagging; Big circles; Holding the breath
Cautions: Safe for backs when form is held. Shoulder pain: reduce range.
Progress by: Bigger circles, feet narrower
Regress by: Swiss ball plank hold
Default prescription: 2 to 3 x 5 to 10 circles each way | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill

FAMILY side_plank | Core: anti lateral flexion (side plank)
Muscles: Obliques, quadratus lumborum, glute med
Why: Part of the McGill Big 3. Builds the side core that stabilises the spine when carrying or standing on one leg. Hip abduction versions also train the glute med.
Cues: 1) Elbow under the shoulder, body in a straight line 2) Push the hips up and forward 3) Breathe and hold
Faults: Hips sagging or piking back; Rolling forward; Shoulder shrugging
Cautions: Shoulder pain: kneeling version or suitcase carry instead.
Progress by: Feet stacked, add hip abduction, Copenhagen
Regress by: Knees bent side plank
Default prescription: 3 sets x 15 to 45s each side (or 6/4/2 x 10s) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Squat University

FAMILY pallof | Core: anti rotation (Pallof press)
Muscles: Obliques, deep core, glutes
Why: The core's main job is to stop unwanted movement. The Pallof press resists the cable pulling you round, training the anti rotation strength that protects the back and transfers force in golf, tennis and lifting (McGill, Boyle, Cosgrove).
Cues: 1) Side on to the cable at chest height, brace 2) Press the handle straight out from the chest and hold 2s 3) Don't let the hands or hips rotate, return to the chest
Faults: Rotating towards the cable; Leaning away; Holding the breath
Cautions: Excellent for back pain. Half kneeling reduces hip involvement and isolates the core.
Progress by: Tall kneeling → half kneeling → split → standing → walkouts, add overhead reach, heavier
Regress by: Lighter band, closer to the anchor, iso hold
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Boyle, Cosgrove

FAMILY bird_dog | Core: anti rotation / anti extension (bird dog)
Muscles: Spinal extensors, glutes, deep core, shoulder stabilisers
Why: Part of the McGill Big 3. Reaching the opposite arm and leg while keeping the spine still trains the back extensors and anti rotation control with very low spinal load. A safe daily exercise for back pain and the foundation of Paul Chek's horse stance progressions.
Cues: 1) Hands under shoulders, knees under hips, neutral spine 2) Reach the opposite arm and leg long (not high), hold 8 to 10s 3) Sweep back under without the hips rotating
Faults: Lifting the leg too high and arching; Hips rotating; Rushing
Cautions: Back pain friendly. Wrist pain: use fists.
Progress by: Add a band, hold longer, bird dog row
Regress by: Arm only or leg only
Default prescription: Descending pyramid 6, 4, 2 each side with 8 to 10s holds, or 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Paul Chek, Squat University

FAMILY breathing | Core: breathing and pelvic control
Muscles: Diaphragm, deep core, pelvic floor
Why: Breathing is the foundation of bracing (Chek: breathing first). Crocodile and 90/90 breathing teach 360 degree breathing, calm the nervous system and help back pain and pelvic floor function.
Cues: 1) Lie in position, hands on the lower ribs 2) Breathe in through the nose expanding 360 degrees (belly, sides, back) 3) Exhale slowly fully, feeling the ribs drop
Faults: Chest and shoulder breathing; Arching the back; Rushing
Cautions: Universally safe. Great on RED readiness days.
Progress by: Add movement (dead bug with breathing)
Regress by: Shorter sets
Default prescription: 5 to 10 slow breaths | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Paul Chek, McGill, Squat University

FAMILY pike_core | Core: dynamic flexion / pike
Muscles: Abs, hip flexors, shoulders
Why: Dynamic core exercises that pull the knees or hips up from a plank or lying position. Good for conditioning and variety at higher levels.
Cues: 1) Start in a strong plank 2) Pull the knees or hips up with the abs 3) Return with control
Faults: Sagging plank; Using momentum; Shoulders drifting back
Cautions: Lying V ups and tuck ups flex the spine: avoid for back pain and osteoporosis.
Progress by: Pike instead of tuck
Regress by: Knee tuck, plank
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Boyle, Cosgrove

FAMILY side_bend | Core: lateral flexion
Muscles: Obliques, QL
Why: Side bends train the obliques through range. Suitcase holds/carries train the same muscles isometrically and are usually a better choice.
Cues: 1) Stand tall, weight in one hand 2) Bend to the side slowly, then pull back up with the other side 3) No twisting
Faults: Twisting; Going heavy; Leaning forward
Cautions: Light only for back pain.
Progress by: Heavier, cable
Regress by: Suitcase hold
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Poliquin

FAMILY leg_raise | Core: lower abs / hip flexors
Muscles: Abs, hip flexors
Why: Lowering and raising the legs while keeping the low back down trains lower abdominal control. Hanging versions add grip and are advanced.
Cues: 1) Low back pressed down 2) Lower the legs only as far as the back stays flat 3) Exhale as you lift
Faults: Back arching off the floor; Swinging; Rushing
Cautions: Back pain: keep the knees bent and range short. Hanging versions need healthy shoulders.
Progress by: Straighter legs, hanging
Regress by: Bent knee heel taps, dead bug
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Boyle, Paul Chek

FAMILY bear | Core: quadruped (bear hold / crawl)
Muscles: Core, shoulders, quads, coordination
Why: Bear holds and crawls train the core and shoulders together and build coordination (Gambetta, Chek). Getting down to and moving on the floor is also a longevity skill.
Cues: 1) Hands under shoulders, knees under hips, knees hover 2 to 3cm 2) Back flat, move opposite hand and foot together 3) Small steps, keep the hips still
Faults: Hips high; Back rounding; Moving too fast
Cautions: Wrist pain: use fists or DB handles.
Progress by: Longer, add drag/load, reverse and lateral
Regress by: Static bear hold, knees down
Default prescription: 3 x 20 to 40s or 10 to 20m | Sessions: HYBRID, BURN, STRONG
Sources: Gambetta, Paul Chek

FAMILY russian_twist | Core: rotation with flexion
Muscles: Obliques, abs
Why: Popular rotation exercises that combine spinal flexion and twisting. They do train the obliques, but the combination of flexion and rotation is the riskiest for discs.
Cues: 1) Sit tall, lean back slightly 2) Rotate from the ribcage, not just the arms 3) Control every rep
Faults: Rounded slumped spine; Fast swinging; Heavy load
Cautions: AVOID for back pain, disc history and osteoporosis (McGill). Prefer Pallof press and chops.
Progress by: Feet up, heavier
Regress by: Feet down, no load, Pallof press instead
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID
Sources: Paul Chek (ball versions), McGill (caution)

FAMILY crunch | Core: spinal flexion (crunch / sit up)
Muscles: Abs
Why: Classic ab exercises. They work the abs but repeated loaded spinal flexion is not ideal for discs, so they are low priority at Optimal compared with anti movement work.
Cues: 1) Ribs towards the hips 2) Exhale as you curl 3) Control the lowering
Faults: Pulling on the neck; Using momentum; High reps fast
Cautions: AVOID for back pain, disc history and osteoporosis. Use McGill curl up instead.
Progress by: Weighted, cable
Regress by: McGill curl up, dead bug
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: BURN
Sources: McGill (caution), Boyle (anti movement preference)

FAMILY rdl | Hinge: Romanian deadlift
Muscles: Hamstrings, glutes, spinal erectors, grip
Why: The RDL starts from the top and lowers the weight by pushing the hips back, so it builds hamstrings and glutes in a lengthened position with low skill demand. It's the best hamstring builder for most members and a staple for posture and back resilience.
Cues: 1) Soft knees, weight close to the legs 2) Push the hips back, slide the weight down the thighs, keep the back flat 3) Stop when the hamstrings are tight (usually mid shin), drive the hips forward to stand
Faults: Rounding the back to go lower; Squatting it by bending the knees too much; Weight drifting away from the legs
Cautions: Back friendly with good form and controlled range. Reduce range for tight hamstrings or low back sensitivity.
Progress by: DB → double KB → trap bar → barbell RDL, tempo (4s lowering), pauses, deficit
Regress by: KB deadlift from blocks, cable pull through, hinge dowel drill, reduce range
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Poliquin, Cosgrove, DeFranco

FAMILY back_ext | Hinge: back extension
Muscles: Spinal erectors, glutes, hamstrings
Why: Strengthens the back extensors and glutes, improving posture and back resilience. Reverse hypers decompress the spine as they train. Extend to neutral only: the goal is a strong, straight spine, not arching.
Cues: 1) Hinge at the hips, keep the spine long 2) Lift to a straight line from head to heels using the glutes 3) Lower with control
Faults: Hyperextending at the top; Swinging/using momentum; Rounding heavily at the bottom under load
Cautions: Good for most back pain when kept to neutral. Avoid loaded flexion at the bottom for osteoporosis.
Progress by: Hold a plate, single leg, pause at the top
Regress by: Superman, bird dog, shorter range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: DeFranco (reverse hyper), McGill, Paul Chek (prone cobra)

FAMILY kb_swing | Hinge: ballistic (power)
Muscles: Glutes, hamstrings, core, grip, cardiovascular system
Why: The king of hinge conditioning (Dan John, Pavel). Trains explosive hip extension (power) and heart rate at the same time with no impact on the knees. Excellent for fat loss, posture and glute strength. Dead stop versions teach control; single arm versions add anti rotation.
Cues: 1) Hike the bell back high between the thighs 2) Snap the hips forward, squeeze the glutes, let the bell float to chest height 3) Let it fall, hinge late, keep the spine neutral
Faults: Squatting the swing instead of hinging; Lifting with the arms; Leaning back at the top
Cautions: Back friendly when the hinge is good. Teach the KB deadlift first. Not for acute low back pain. American (overhead) swings stress shoulders and low back: use hip height swings instead.
Progress by: Heavier bell, single arm, alternating, swings EMOM, heavier with fewer reps for power
Regress by: KB deadlift, dead stop swing, lighter bell, cable pull through
Default prescription: 4 to 10 sets x 10 to 20 reps, or EMOM; 'hike, snap, float' | Sessions: BURN, HYBRID, STRONG
Sources: Dan John, Pavel Tsatsouline, Cosgrove

FAMILY barbell_deadlift | Hinge: barbell deadlift
Muscles: Glutes, hamstrings, back, grip, whole body
Why: The heaviest total body strength lift. Builds posterior chain strength, grip and bone density (LIFTMOR). Block and rack pulls shorten the range for beginners or back sensitivity; deficit and paused versions are advanced.
Cues: 1) Bar over the middle of the foot, shins close 2) Grip, chest up, pull the slack out, brace hard 3) Push the floor away, bar stays close, lock out with the glutes
Faults: Rounding the back; Bar drifting away from the legs; Hyperextending at the top
Cautions: Highest low back demand. Not for active back pain. Trap bar is the default for most 40+ members. Teams G4 only for straight bar.
Progress by: Add load, pause off the floor, deficit, rep max progressions
Regress by: Block/rack pull, trap bar deadlift, KB deadlift
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: DeFranco, LIFTMOR research, Poliquin

FAMILY kb_deadlift | Hinge: deadlift (KB/DB/landmine)
Muscles: Glutes, hamstrings, back, grip
Why: The safest way to teach lifting from the floor. Picking things up is a life skill (Primal Pattern 'bend'). Raising the bell on blocks reduces range for beginners and back pain; suitcase versions add anti lateral flexion.
Cues: 1) Bell between the feet (or beside for suitcase), hinge down with a flat back 2) Grip, pull the shoulders down, brace 3) Push the floor away and stand tall, squeeze the glutes
Faults: Squatting down too low; Rounding to reach the bell; Leaning back at the top
Cautions: Very back friendly. Elevate the bell on a step for limited hip mobility or back history.
Progress by: Heavier bell, double KB, lower to the floor, progress to RDL, swing and trap bar
Regress by: Bell on a block/step, hinge drill
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Dan John, McGill, Cosgrove

FAMILY good_morning | Hinge: good morning
Muscles: Hamstrings, glutes, spinal erectors
Why: Load sits on the upper back while the hips hinge, which strengthens the hamstrings and back extensors. Bodyweight and banded versions are great hinge teaching tools; barbell versions are advanced. ATG style seated good mornings build hamstring and back mobility.
Cues: 1) Soft knees, brace, push the hips back 2) Keep the spine long and neutral 3) Stand up by driving the hips forward
Faults: Rounding the spine; Bending the knees too much; Going deeper than hamstring length allows
Cautions: Loaded good mornings are not for low back pain. Keep loads light and range controlled for 40+.
Progress by: Band → light barbell → SSB good morning, tempo, pause
Regress by: Bodyweight, dowel hinge drill
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Westside/DeFranco, Ben Patrick (seated), Poliquin

FAMILY hip_thrust | Hinge: hip extension (bridge / thrust)
Muscles: Glutes, hamstrings
Why: Bridges and hip thrusts train the glutes at their strongest point (full hip extension) with almost no spinal load. They are essential for back pain, knee pain, posture, sprinting and golf power, and are safe for nearly everyone. Single leg versions fix imbalances.
Cues: 1) Feet flat, shins vertical at the top 2) Tuck the ribs down, drive through the heels and squeeze the glutes to lift 3) Pause at the top without arching the low back, lower slowly
Faults: Arching the low back instead of extending the hips; Feet too far away (all hamstrings); Pushing through the toes
Cautions: Very joint friendly. Use a pad on the hips when loaded. Shoulder elevated versions need a stable bench.
Progress by: Bridge → hip thrust off a bench → load (DB, barbell) → single leg, add pauses or bands
Regress by: Floor bridge, shorter range, isometric hold
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 (pause 1 to 2s at the top) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Cosgrove, McGill, Boyle

FAMILY pull_through | Hinge: hip extension accessory
Muscles: Glutes, hamstrings
Why: Cable/band pull throughs teach the hip hinge with the load pulling from behind, so people naturally sit the hips back and finish with the glutes. Kickbacks and quadruped hip extensions isolate the glutes for activation and rehab.
Cues: 1) Face away from the cable, rope between the legs 2) Hinge back letting the rope pull the hips back 3) Drive the hips through and squeeze the glutes, stand tall
Faults: Pulling with the arms; Squatting instead of hinging; Leaning back at the top
Cautions: Very back friendly. A great swing and deadlift regression.
Progress by: Heavier, pause at the top, progress to KB swing
Regress by: Band version, shorter range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Cosgrove, McGill, Boyle

FAMILY ham_curl | Hinge: knee flexion (hamstring curl)
Muscles: Hamstrings (knee flexion function), glutes
Why: Hamstrings both extend the hip and bend the knee; curls train the knee bending role that RDLs miss. Strong hamstrings protect knees and reduce hamstring strain risk. Nordic curls are among the best researched injury prevention exercises.
Cues: 1) Lie on your back, heels on the ball/sliders/TRX, lift the hips 2) Curl the heels towards the bum keeping the hips high 3) Extend slowly back out (control is the exercise)
Faults: Hips dropping during the curl; Rushing the lowering; Cramping (start with fewer reps)
Cautions: Knee friendly. Nordics are demanding: use band assistance and partial range, expect soreness at first.
Progress by: Hips stay up throughout, single leg, slower lowering, Nordic progression
Regress by: Hips on the floor curl, eccentric only, double leg
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Squat University, Ben Patrick (Nordic), Poliquin

FAMILY jefferson | Hinge: loaded spinal flexion (mobility)
Muscles: Spinal erectors and hamstrings in a lengthened position
Why: Ben Patrick's loaded spinal flexion drill: very light load, rolling down one vertebra at a time to build strength and tolerance in flexion. Used for back resilience and hamstring flexibility in healthy, well trained people.
Cues: 1) Stand on a box with a very light weight 2) Tuck the chin and roll down slowly, one segment at a time 3) Roll back up from the bottom, slow and controlled
Faults: Going heavy; Rushing; Doing it when the back is irritated
Cautions: CONTRAINDICATED for osteoporosis/osteopenia, disc problems, acute back pain and most beginners. G4 only, Teams, coach approved.
Progress by: Tiny load increases only after months
Regress by: Seated good morning (ATG), cat cow, toe touch to squat
Default prescription: 2 sets x 5 to 8 very slow reps, bodyweight or very light | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG)

FAMILY hinge_drill | Hinge: pattern teaching
Muscles: Hamstrings, glutes, spinal control
Why: Teaches people to move at the hips not the spine. The dowel gives instant feedback (head, upper back and tailbone stay in contact). Every hinge exercise depends on this skill, so McGill and Squat University teach it first.
Cues: 1) Dowel along the spine touching head, upper back and tailbone 2) Soft knees, push the hips back as if closing a car door with your bum 3) Hinge until you feel the hamstrings, keep all three contact points, stand back up
Faults: Losing the dowel contact at the low back; Bending the knees into a squat; Looking up and cranking the neck
Cautions: Ideal for back pain members. No load, no risk.
Progress by: Hold a KB at the chest, progress to KB deadlift from blocks
Regress by: Hinge to a wall (bum touches the wall) with a smaller range
Default prescription: 2 sets x 8 to 10 slow reps (warm up) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: McGill, Squat University, Dan John

FAMILY sl_rdl | Hinge: single leg / staggered
Muscles: Hamstrings, glutes (incl. glute med), foot and ankle stability
Why: Single leg hinges build hamstring and glute strength, balance and hip stability one side at a time, and they spare the spine because loads are lighter. Kickstand and B stance versions give a stepping stone with the back foot for support. Hip airplanes train hip rotation control (great for golf).
Cues: 1) Stand on one leg with a soft knee, other leg reaching back 2) Hinge forward as one straight line from head to heel, hips square 3) Drive the standing heel into the floor and squeeze the glute to stand
Faults: Hips opening up (rotating) towards the ceiling; Rounding the back to reach the floor; Standing knee locked straight
Cautions: Back friendly. Balance limits load: use a support or kickstand first. Excellent for golfers and runners.
Progress by: Kickstand → full single leg, add load (DB/KB, double KB, landmine), slower tempo, add a row or reach
Regress by: Supported single leg RDL (hand on rig), kickstand RDL, bodyweight
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 (each leg) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Boyle, Cosgrove, Gambetta

FAMILY trap_bar | Hinge: trap bar deadlift
Muscles: Glutes, quads, hamstrings, back, grip
Why: The trap bar lets people stand inside the weight with neutral grip, so the load sits in line with the body. Far less low back stress than a straight bar and much easier to learn. DeFranco and Cosgrove's go to heavy lift and Optimal's default heavy deadlift for 40+ (bone density, strength, grip).
Cues: 1) Stand in the middle, hinge down and grip the handles 2) Chest proud, take the slack out of the bar, brace 3) Push the floor away, stand tall, lower with control
Faults: Hips shooting up first; Rounding the back off the floor; Jerking the bar off the floor
Cautions: The most joint friendly heavy lift. High handles reduce range for tall members or stiff hips/backs.
Progress by: Add load, low handles, pause just off the floor, rep max progressions (Teams G4)
Regress by: High handles, KB deadlift from blocks, lighter load
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: DeFranco, Cosgrove, LIFTMOR research

FAMILY abduction | Hip: abduction / glute med
Muscles: Glute medius and minimus, hip stabilisers
Why: The side glutes stop the knee caving and the pelvis dropping when on one leg. Weak glute meds are linked to knee pain, hip pain and back pain. Simple band work fixes it and works brilliantly as a warm up (Squat University, Boyle).
Cues: 1) Athletic stance, band around the knees or ankles 2) Push the knees out, step sideways (or lift the leg) leading with the heel 3) Stay low and keep the pelvis level
Faults: Rocking the torso side to side; Feet turning out; Letting the band pull the knees in
Cautions: Very joint friendly, ideal for hip and knee rehab.
Progress by: Heavier band, band lower on the leg, side plank abduction
Regress by: Side lying with no band, smaller range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 20 each side | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, Boyle, Paul Chek

FAMILY copenhagen | Hip: adductor strength
Muscles: Adductors (inner thigh), core
Why: Copenhagen planks are among the best researched ways to prevent groin injury, and adductor strength supports knee and hip health. Ball squeezes are a gentle entry point.
Cues: 1) Side plank with the top leg on a bench (inside of the knee for short lever, ankle for long lever) 2) Lift the hips into a straight line by squeezing the top leg down 3) Hold or lift and lower
Faults: Hips sagging; Rolling forward; Starting with the long lever
Cautions: Short lever first. Groin pain: ball squeeze isometrics.
Progress by: Short → long lever, add leg raises
Regress by: Ball squeeze, side lying adduction
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 15 to 30s each side | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Research (groin injury prevention), Boyle

FAMILY hip_flexor | Hip: hip flexor strength
Muscles: Hip flexors (psoas, rectus femoris)
Why: Hip flexor strength (especially lifting the knee above 90 degrees) declines with age and affects walking, stairs and tripping. Ben Patrick includes it in ATG Zero.
Cues: 1) Sit or lie tall 2) Lift the knee up without leaning back 3) Lower slowly
Faults: Leaning back; Arching the low back; Momentum
Cautions: Gentle and safe. Hip impingement: keep below pain.
Progress by: Band resistance, standing, longer holds
Regress by: Smaller range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG), Rehab practice

FAMILY hip_rotation | Hip: rotation mobility
Muscles: Hip rotators, capsule
Why: Hip internal and external rotation are often the first ranges lost with age and are critical for the golf swing, squat depth and low back health (joint by joint).
Cues: 1) Sit tall in 90/90 or lie in position 2) Rotate through the hips, keep the chest tall 3) Move slowly, hold the end range
Faults: Rounding the back; Forcing; Using hands to cheat
Cautions: Hip pain: reduce range.
Progress by: Hands free, hip lifts, longer holds
Regress by: Hands support
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, Gambetta (golf)

FAMILY inverted_row | Horizontal pull: inverted / suspension row
Muscles: Upper back, lats, rear shoulders, biceps, core
Why: Pulling your own bodyweight in a plank position. The angle sets the difficulty, so it scales from total beginner (standing upright) to advanced (feet elevated) in the same station. A core Optimal group exercise and part of Ed's screening (TRX row at 45 degrees).
Cues: 1) Body in a straight line, heels down 2) Pull the chest to the handles, shoulder blades back and down 3) Lower slowly to straight arms
Faults: Hips sagging; Shrugging or poking the chin forward; Half reps
Cautions: Shoulder friendly. Neutral grip TRX handles are easiest on elbows.
Progress by: Walk the feet forward (more horizontal), feet elevated, single arm, pause/tempo
Regress by: Stand more upright, step back, scapular retraction only (G1 of the Optimal screen)
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 15 reps (or total reps progression), pause at the top | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Cosgrove, Boyle, Dan John

FAMILY row | Horizontal pull: row
Muscles: Upper back, lats, rear shoulders, biceps, grip
Why: Rows build the upper back that holds posture upright and balances pressing. Chest supported rows remove the low back from the equation; single arm rows allow heavier loading and fix imbalances; bent over rows add hinge endurance. Aim for about 2 pulls for every push in desk based members.
Cues: 1) Brace, shoulder blade pulls first, then the elbow 2) Row towards the hip (lats) or lower ribs (upper back) 3) Pause, lower slowly to a full stretch
Faults: Jerking and twisting the torso; Shrugging; Shortening the range
Cautions: Chest supported and cable versions are the most back friendly. Bent over versions load the low back: avoid pairing with RDLs for 40+.
Progress by: Heavier, pause at the top, tempo, single arm, 1.5 reps
Regress by: Chest supported row, cable row, TRX row
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: DeFranco, Cressey, Poliquin, Boyle

FAMILY db_press | Horizontal push: DB/KB press
Muscles: Chest, front shoulders, triceps
Why: Dumbbells let each arm move freely and the shoulders find their own path, so they are friendlier for 40+ shoulders than a straight bar. Neutral grip (palms facing) is the most shoulder friendly; squeeze presses bias the chest with lighter loads; single arm versions add core.
Cues: 1) Shoulder blades back and down 2) Lower with elbows about 45 degrees, forearms vertical 3) Press up together without clanking the bells
Faults: Flaring elbows; Dropping too deep with tight shoulders; Arching the low back off the bench
Cautions: Neutral grip and floor presses protect shoulders. Single arm versions: brace hard.
Progress by: Heavier, single arm, alternating, tempo, incline angle changes
Regress by: Floor press, lighter weight, push up variation
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Poliquin, Cosgrove, Cressey

FAMILY cable_press | Horizontal push: cable / band press
Muscles: Chest, shoulders, triceps, core (standing)
Why: Standing presses with cables or bands train pushing while the core resists rotation and extension, closer to real life pushing. Smooth, joint friendly resistance.
Cues: 1) Split or athletic stance, brace 2) Press forward until arms are straight, keep the ribs down 3) Return with control
Faults: Leaning into the press; Rotating the torso; Shrugging
Cautions: Very shoulder friendly.
Progress by: Single arm, heavier, slower, split stance to parallel stance
Regress by: Two arms, lighter, wider stance
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Boyle, Cosgrove

FAMILY fly | Horizontal push: fly (isolation)
Muscles: Chest, front shoulders
Why: Flys isolate the chest by moving the arm across the body with the elbow fixed. Useful for muscle building and variety at lighter loads.
Cues: 1) Slight bend in the elbows, kept fixed 2) Open the arms with control until a stretch across the chest 3) Hug back together, squeezing the chest
Faults: Going too deep and stressing the shoulder; Bending and straightening the elbows (turns into a press); Going too heavy
Cautions: Limit range for shoulder pain. Cables are smoother than DBs.
Progress by: Cable, slower tempo, pauses
Regress by: Lighter, shorter range, band fly
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Poliquin, Schoenfeld (hypertrophy)

FAMILY barbell_bench | Horizontal push: press (bench / floor)
Muscles: Chest, front shoulders, triceps
Why: Pressing builds upper body strength and muscle. DB versions allow a natural path for the shoulders; floor presses limit range, which protects shoulders (a DeFranco favourite for cranky shoulders). Barbell bench is a G4 strength option.
Cues: 1) Shoulder blades pulled back and down, feet planted 2) Lower with control to the chest (or until elbows touch the floor) 3) Press up and slightly back, keep the wrists stacked over the elbows
Faults: Flared elbows at 90 degrees; Bouncing off the chest; Losing the shoulder blade position
Cautions: Shoulder pain: floor press, neutral grip DBs, or landmine press. Wrists stacked.
Progress by: Add load, pause on the chest, rep max on rotating variations (Teams G4)
Regress by: DB floor press, neutral grip DB press, push up
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: DeFranco, Poliquin, Cosgrove

FAMILY push_up | Horizontal push: push up
Muscles: Chest, shoulders, triceps, serratus, core (anti extension)
Why: A moving plank: chest and arm strength plus core control. The shoulder blades move freely, which is healthier for shoulders than bench pressing. Easy to scale by hand height so every level in a group can do the same exercise. Push up plus adds serratus work for shoulder health.
Cues: 1) Hands just outside shoulders, body in a straight line, glutes and abs tight 2) Lower the chest between the hands, elbows about 45 degrees from the body 3) Push the floor away until arms are straight and the upper back is spread
Faults: Hips sagging; Elbows flaring to 90 degrees; Head poking forward / half reps
Cautions: Wrist pain: use DB handles or fists. Shoulder pain: elevate the hands and keep elbows tucked.
Progress by: Lower the hand height (wall → bench → box → floor), tempo, pause, feet elevated, add a weighted vest/plate
Regress by: Higher incline (bench or wall), knee push up, shorter range
Default prescription: 3 sets x 6 to 15 reps (or total reps progression) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Cosgrove, Boyle, Dan John

FAMILY dip | Horizontal/vertical push: dip
Muscles: Triceps, chest, front shoulders
Why: Strong triceps and chest builder, but the bottom position stretches the front of the shoulder under load. Use selectively in the 40+ population.
Cues: 1) Shoulders down and back, lean slightly forward 2) Lower until upper arms are about parallel (or less if painful) 3) Press up to straight arms
Faults: Going too deep; Shoulders rolling forward; Bench dips with the shoulder internally rotated
Cautions: Bench dips and deep dips often irritate shoulders. Avoid with any shoulder pain. Assisted versions first.
Progress by: Assisted → bodyweight → weighted
Regress by: Band assisted, close grip press up, tricep extensions
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: DeFranco, Poliquin

FAMILY backward_sled | Knee: backward walking / sled
Muscles: Quads (VMO), knee tendons
Why: Ben Patrick's number one knee tool: walking backwards loads the quads with the knee travelling over the toes, floods the knee with blood and is nearly impossible to overdo.
Cues: 1) Lean back slightly against the sled (or hold the treadmill rails) 2) Small steps, push through the front of the foot 3) Knees travel over the toes
Faults: Big steps; Standing tall; Too heavy
Cautions: Knee pain staple. Very safe.
Progress by: Heavier, longer
Regress by: Lighter, treadmill flat
Default prescription: 3 to 5 min warm up, or 4 to 6 x 20m | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG)

FAMILY quad_iso | Knee: quad activation
Muscles: Quads (VMO)
Why: First stage quad work for knee pain, after surgery and for replacements. Terminal knee extensions train the last part of straightening the knee, which is often lost.
Cues: 1) Tighten the thigh to straighten the knee 2) Hold 3 to 5s 3) Lower slowly
Faults: Rushing; Bent knee in straight leg raise; Lifting too high
Cautions: Rehab friendly.
Progress by: Add ankle weight, band
Regress by: No load
Default prescription: 2 to 3 x 10 to 15, 3s holds | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Rehab practice, Poliquin (VMO)

FAMILY heel_slide | Knee: range of motion
Muscles: Knee flexion range
Why: Gentle knee bending range work, used after injury or surgery.
Cues: 1) Slide the heel towards the bum slowly 2) Stop at gentle stretch 3) Return
Faults: Forcing; Rushing; Holding breath
Cautions: Rehab friendly.
Progress by: Bigger range
Regress by: Smaller range
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Rehab practice

FAMILY lateral_step | Lateral: step up / step over
Muscles: Glutes, quads, adductors
Why: Lateral step ups train single leg strength in the frontal plane, great for knee control and balance.
Cues: 1) Side on to the box, foot fully on 2) Drive up through the box leg 3) Lower with control
Faults: Pushing off the floor leg; Knee caving; Box too high
Cautions: Knee friendly at low heights.
Progress by: Higher box, add load, knee drive
Regress by: Lower box, hand support
Default prescription: 3 sets x 8 to 12 reps, tempo 3-0-1-1, rest 60 to 90s, RPE 7 to 9 each side | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Gambetta, Boyle

FAMILY calf | Lower leg: calf
Muscles: Calves (gastrocnemius straight knee, soleus bent knee), Achilles
Why: Strong calves protect the knee and Achilles and power walking. Ben Patrick's ATG insists on full range (stretch at the bottom). Bent knee versions train the soleus, critical for running and walking endurance.
Cues: 1) Full stretch at the bottom 2) Rise to the highest point on the big toe 3) Pause, lower slowly (3s)
Faults: Bouncing; Rolling to the outside of the foot; Partial range
Cautions: Achilles pain: slow heavy calf raises are rehab gold.
Progress by: Single leg, off a step, add load
Regress by: Two legs, hand support, flat floor
Default prescription: 3 x 12 to 20 (slow) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG), Rehab research (Achilles)

FAMILY tib_raise | Lower leg: tibialis raise
Muscles: Tibialis anterior (shin), ankle
Why: Ben Patrick's 'bottom up' foundation: the shin muscle balances the calf, decelerates the foot and protects the knee. Wall tib raises take 1 minute and belong in every lower body warm up.
Cues: 1) Back against a wall, heels 30 to 50cm away 2) Lift the toes up as high as possible 3) Lower slowly
Faults: Bending the knees; Rushing; Partial range
Cautions: Universally safe.
Progress by: Feet further from the wall, single leg, tib bar load
Regress by: Closer to the wall
Default prescription: 2 to 3 x 20 to 25 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Ben Patrick (ATG)

FAMILY ankle_mob | Mobility: ankle dorsiflexion
Muscles: Ankle joint, calves
Why: Stiff ankles cause heels lifting, knees caving and forward lean in squats, and pain further up the chain (joint by joint). The knee to wall test is the screen; rocks and banded mobs are the fix (Squat University).
Cues: 1) Half kneeling, foot about 10cm from the wall 2) Drive the knee forward over the toes keeping the heel down 3) Rock in and out
Faults: Heel lifting; Knee caving inward; Forcing
Cautions: Universally safe.
Progress by: Further from the wall, band, load
Regress by: Closer to the wall
Default prescription: 2 x 10 to 15 rocks each side | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, Ben Patrick

FAMILY cars | Mobility: controlled articular rotations (CARs)
Muscles: Joint capsule, end range control
Why: Slow, full range joint circles under tension. Maintains each joint's usable range and gives a daily joint health check.
Cues: 1) Brace everything else 2) Move the joint through its biggest circle slowly 3) Keep tension the whole way
Faults: Compensating with other joints; Rushing; Forcing painful range
Cautions: Universally safe.
Progress by: Bigger circle, more tension
Regress by: Smaller circle
Default prescription: 3 to 5 slow reps each direction | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: FRC style mobility, Dean Somerset

FAMILY spinal_mob | Mobility: spinal / back care
Muscles: Spine, hips
Why: Gentle spinal mobility for back care and warm ups. Extension drills (prone lying, McKenzie press ups) often ease disc type back pain; flexion drills (knee to chest, child's pose) often ease stiffness and stenosis type pain. Find the direction that helps the individual (McGill: find the pain triggers) and use it.
Cues: 1) Move slowly through comfortable range 2) Breathe out into the end range 3) Stop if symptoms travel down a leg
Faults: Forcing range; Holding the breath; Bouncing
Cautions: Generally very safe. Osteoporosis: avoid repeated or loaded spinal flexion. Pain spreading into a leg: stop and refer.
Progress by: Bigger range, add breathing holds
Regress by: Smaller range
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: McGill, Squat University, McKenzie method (physio)

FAMILY stretch | Mobility: stretch / spinal mobility
Muscles: Target muscles and joints vary (named in the exercise)
Why: Static and gentle mobility work restores range lost from sitting and ageing. Best used where a screen shows a restriction (Squat University: screen, correct, retest), and to cool down. McKenzie press ups and extension work often relieve disc type back pain; flexion stretches often suit stenosis type pain: individualise.
Cues: 1) Get into position slowly 2) Breathe out to relax into range, no bouncing 3) Hold 30 to 60s or do slow reps
Faults: Forcing painful range; Holding the breath; Stretching instead of strengthening when stability is the issue
Cautions: Gentle and safe. Stop if symptoms travel down the leg. Osteoporosis: avoid loaded flexion stretches.
Progress by: Longer holds, deeper range, add loaded stretching
Regress by: Smaller range, supported versions
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, DeFranco (Limber 11), Ben Patrick (couch stretch), McGill

FAMILY tspine | Mobility: thoracic spine
Muscles: Thoracic spine rotation and extension, lats, pecs
Why: The upper back should move; the low back should be stable (joint by joint). Stiff upper backs cause rounded posture, shoulder pain and low back pain, and limit the golf swing. Daily T spine drills fix it.
Cues: 1) Keep the hips still (knee pinned or in quadruped) 2) Rotate/extend through the upper back, follow the hand with the eyes 3) Breathe out into the end range
Faults: Rotating from the low back; Rushing; Forcing range
Cautions: Very safe. Osteoporosis: avoid forced flexion; gentle rotation and extension are fine.
Progress by: Add a reach, hold end range longer
Regress by: Smaller range, seated version
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, Paul Chek, Cressey

FAMILY olympic | Power: Olympic lift / derivative
Muscles: Whole body: glutes, quads, hamstrings, traps, shoulders
Why: Explosive triple extension (ankles, knees, hips) trains power and rate of force development. Power declines faster than strength with age, so it must be trained, but the skill demand is high. DB, KB and landmine versions are far easier to learn and safer for groups than barbell versions.
Cues: 1) Start in a strong hinge with the weight close 2) Explode up through the legs and hips, shrug and pull the elbows high 3) Catch softly in a strong rack position (or overhead), stand tall
Faults: Pulling early with the arms; Weight swinging away from the body; Catching with the back arched
Cautions: Wrists, shoulders and low back need to be healthy. Barbell versions: Teams G4 only. Not for osteoporosis without careful progression.
Progress by: Pull → hang version → from the floor; DB → KB → barbell
Regress by: Pull only (no catch), KB swing, med ball scoop toss, landmine clean
Default prescription: 3 to 5 sets x 3 to 6 reps, full recovery (60 to 120s), stop when speed drops | Sessions: HYBRID, STRONG
Sources: DeFranco, Cosgrove (power block), Gambetta

FAMILY jump | Power: jumps, hops and landings (plyometric)
Muscles: Whole lower body, tendons, nervous system
Why: Jumps and hops train power, tendon stiffness and bone (impact is a key bone stimulus in LIFTMOR). Landings (snap downs, drop squats, jump and stick) teach the body to absorb force, the foundation of injury prevention. Progress carefully: landing before jumping, low before high, two legs before one.
Cues: 1) Land softly and quietly, hips back, knees over toes 2) Stick the landing for a second before the next rep 3) Quality over quantity: stop when height or control drops
Faults: Landing stiff legged or with knees caving; Too many reps (plyos are not cardio); Progressing too fast
Cautions: IMPACT: excluded from Optimal GROUP sessions (no jumping rule). Teams only after screening. Avoid with pelvic floor symptoms, osteoporosis with fracture history, joint replacements, acute knee/Achilles pain.
Progress by: Landings → small jumps → box jumps → broad jumps → single leg hops → depth jumps
Regress by: Snap downs/drop squat and stick, step ups, sled push, med ball throws
Group swap: Group swap (no jumping): sled push, fast step ups, med ball chest pass or slam, KB swing, fast sit to stand
Default prescription: 3 to 5 sets x 3 to 6 reps, full recovery (60 to 120s), stop when speed drops; 2 to 3 sessions/week max, low total contacts (20 to 60) | Sessions: HYBRID
Sources: Gambetta, DeFranco (dynamic effort), LIFTMOR research (impact for bone)

FAMILY med_ball | Power: medicine ball throws and slams
Muscles: Whole body power, rotational core (rotational throws), shoulders
Why: Med ball throws are the safest way to train power for 40+ members: no catching heavy loads, no landing impact, and you can throw as hard as possible. Rotational throws (scoop toss, shot put) are THE golf power exercise (Gambetta, Chek). Wall balls combine a squat with a throw for conditioning.
Cues: 1) Load the hips (or rotate back through the hips for rotational throws) 2) Explode from the ground up: legs, hips, then arms 3) Throw with intent every rep, reset between reps
Faults: Throwing with arms only; Rounding the back on slams; Doing so many reps that it becomes slow cardio
Cautions: Low impact and joint friendly. Use a soft ball for slams. Rotational throws: avoid with acute back pain. Wall balls need shoulder overhead range.
Progress by: Heavier ball, more distance/speed, step into the throw, single leg stance
Regress by: Lighter ball, half kneeling throw, slower controlled chops
Default prescription: 3 to 5 sets x 3 to 6 reps, full recovery (60 to 120s), stop when speed drops (each side for rotational) | Sessions: HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Gambetta, Cosgrove (power block), Paul Chek (twist)

FAMILY plyo_push | Power: upper body plyometric
Muscles: Chest, shoulders, triceps
Why: Explosive push ups train upper body power. Advanced and higher joint stress, so Teams only for members with healthy wrists and shoulders.
Cues: 1) Lower under control, push explosively so the hands leave the floor 2) Land softly with bent elbows 3) Reset each rep
Faults: Sagging hips; Landing on locked elbows; Doing them fatigued
Cautions: Wrist and shoulder stress. Not for groups or anyone with shoulder pain. Med ball chest pass is the safer option.
Progress by: Higher, then clap
Regress by: Incline plyo push up, med ball chest pass
Group swap: Group swap: med ball chest pass or explosive band press
Default prescription: 3 to 5 sets x 3 to 6 reps, full recovery (60 to 120s), stop when speed drops | Sessions: HYBRID
Sources: DeFranco, Gambetta

FAMILY plank_row | Pull + core: plank row
Muscles: Upper back, lats, core (anti rotation), shoulders
Why: Rowing from a plank or bear position adds a strong anti rotation challenge: the core must stop the hips twisting. Great for HYBRID and BURN sessions.
Cues: 1) Wide feet, strong plank or bear position 2) Row one weight to the hip without the hips rotating 3) Place it down with control, alternate
Faults: Hips rotating; Feet too narrow; Rushing reps
Cautions: Wrist load: use hex DBs. Shoulder pain: use bird dog row or chest supported row instead.
Progress by: Narrower feet, heavier, add a push up
Regress by: Knees down, bird dog row, chest supported row
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Cosgrove, McGill (anti rotation)

FAMILY face_pull | Pull: face pull (shoulder health)
Muscles: Rear shoulders, rotator cuff (external rotators), mid traps
Why: Face pulls combine a high row with external rotation, strengthening the muscles that keep shoulders healthy and posture upright. Cressey and DeFranco use them in almost every programme to balance all the pressing. Optimal: at least 2x per week for desk workers.
Cues: 1) Rope at forehead height, thumbs pointing back 2) Pull towards the face, elbows high, separate the rope 3) Finish with the knuckles beside the ears (like a double bicep pose), pause
Faults: Leaning back and using bodyweight; Elbows dropping; Shrugging
Cautions: Shoulder friendly and often therapeutic.
Progress by: Heavier, slower, add external rotation emphasis
Regress by: Band face pull, lighter
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 12 to 20 reps, pause 1s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: DeFranco, Cressey, Poliquin (structural balance)

FAMILY pull_apart | Pull: rear delt / upper back accessory
Muscles: Rear shoulders, rhomboids, mid traps
Why: High rep upper back work that counters forward rounded posture. DeFranco has athletes do hundreds per week. Perfect in warm ups and between pressing sets.
Cues: 1) Arms straight in front, slight bend in the elbows 2) Pull the hands apart until the band touches the chest, squeeze the shoulder blades 3) Return slowly
Faults: Shrugging; Bending the elbows to cheat; Arching the back
Cautions: Very shoulder friendly.
Progress by: Heavier band, pauses, different angles (overhand, underhand, diagonal)
Regress by: Lighter band
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 15 to 25 reps | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: DeFranco, Cressey

FAMILY ytw | Pull: scapular / lower trap raises
Muscles: Lower and middle traps, rear shoulders, rotator cuff
Why: Small raises in Y, T, W and I shapes strengthen the muscles that control the shoulder blade, especially the lower traps that are often weak in rounded posture. Poliquin's structural balance and Cressey's shoulder programmes rely on them.
Cues: 1) Chest supported on an incline bench or lying face down 2) Thumbs up, lift the arms into the shape using the shoulder blades 3) Pause 1 to 2s at the top, lower slowly
Faults: Shrugging up to the ears; Using heavy weights and swinging; Arching the low back
Cautions: Shoulder rehab friendly. Use 1 to 3kg or bodyweight.
Progress by: Light DBs, longer pauses
Regress by: Bodyweight, smaller range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 8 to 12 per shape, pause at the top | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Poliquin, Cressey, Squat University

FAMILY shrug | Pull: shrug / upright row
Muscles: Upper traps, shoulders
Why: Traps support the neck and shoulders and carry heavy loads. Upright rows build shoulders but can pinch the shoulder joint: keep them below shoulder height or use cables/bands.
Cues: 1) Stand tall, arms straight 2) Lift the shoulders straight up to the ears (shrug) or elbows to chest height (upright row) 3) Pause, lower slowly
Faults: Rolling the shoulders; Pulling too high in upright rows; Using momentum
Cautions: Upright rows: avoid with shoulder impingement. Shrugs are safe.
Progress by: Heavier, pause at the top
Regress by: Lighter, cable/band
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Poliquin, DeFranco

FAMILY landmine_press | Push: landmine press
Muscles: Shoulders, chest, triceps, serratus, core
Why: The landmine press moves on an arc between a horizontal and vertical press, letting the shoulder blade move freely. It is the most shoulder friendly way to press overhead and the go to for members who can't press straight overhead (Cressey). Half kneeling adds core and hip stability.
Cues: 1) Hold the end of the bar at the shoulder, ribs down, glutes tight 2) Press up and forward, letting the shoulder blade reach around 3) Lower with control back to the shoulder
Faults: Arching the low back; Shrugging; Twisting the torso to press
Cautions: Best press for shoulder pain and limited overhead range.
Progress by: Half kneeling → tall kneeling → split stance → standing → push press; add plates
Regress by: Lighter, two hands, half kneeling
Default prescription: 3 to 4 sets x 6 to 10 reps, tempo 3-1-1-0, rest 90 to 120s, RPE 7 to 8 (each arm) | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Cressey, Boyle, DeFranco

FAMILY chop | Rotation: chop and lift
Muscles: Obliques, core, hips, shoulders
Why: Diagonal patterns that link the hips and shoulders through the core (Paul Chek's twist pattern, Gray Cook's chops and lifts). Train controlled rotation and are a staple of golf programmes.
Cues: 1) Feet stable, brace 2) Pull the cable diagonally across the body, letting the hips and upper back turn together 3) Control the return
Faults: All arms; Twisting only at the low back; Too heavy and jerky
Cautions: Keep rotation through the hips and upper back, not the lumbar spine. Half kneeling is most back friendly.
Progress by: Standing, heavier, with a step, faster (power)
Regress by: Half/tall kneeling, lighter, smaller range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Paul Chek, Gray Cook, Gambetta

FAMILY rotation | Rotation: loaded rotation
Muscles: Obliques, hips, upper back
Why: Controlled rotation through the hips and upper back with the landmine or cable. Builds rotational strength for golf and life (turning to reach, carrying).
Cues: 1) Arms long, pivot the back foot 2) Turn from the hips and upper back together 3) Control the return through the middle
Faults: Rotating only at the low back; Arms bending; Moving too fast
Cautions: Avoid fast loaded rotation with acute back pain.
Progress by: Heavier, faster, split stance
Regress by: Lighter, smaller range, anti rotation instead
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 6 to 10 reps each side (or 20 to 40s), slow and controlled | Sessions: HYBRID, STRONG
Sources: Gambetta, Paul Chek

FAMILY halo | Shoulder / core: halo
Muscles: Shoulders, upper back, core
Why: Circling a weight around the head builds shoulder mobility and core control. Great warm up tool.
Cues: 1) Hold the bell upside down by the horns 2) Circle it close around the head 3) Keep ribs down, don't move the hips
Faults: Arching the back; Bell too far from the head; Going too heavy
Cautions: Gentle for most shoulders.
Progress by: Heavier, half kneeling
Regress by: Lighter, plate
Default prescription: 2 sets x 5 to 8 each way | Sessions: HYBRID, STRONG
Sources: Dan John

FAMILY rhythmic | Shoulder / hip: stability and control
Muscles: Rotator cuff, deep stabilisers
Why: Small fast perturbations or weight shifts train joint stability reflexes.
Cues: 1) Stable position 2) Small controlled movements 3) Keep the joint centred
Faults: Big sloppy movements; Shrugging; Holding the breath
Cautions: Rehab friendly.
Progress by: Longer, less stable
Regress by: Shorter, more support
Default prescription: 3 x 20 to 30s | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Rehab practice

FAMILY pendulum | Shoulder mobility: gentle range
Muscles: Shoulder joint, capsule, lats, pecs
Why: Gentle shoulder range of motion work. Pendulums and table slides are first stage rehab for stiff or painful shoulders; dowel and band pass throughs maintain overhead and behind body mobility (DeFranco Simple 6).
Cues: 1) Relaxed, no forcing 2) Move slowly through comfortable range 3) Breathe
Faults: Forcing painful range; Shrugging; Arching to fake range
Cautions: Use pain free range only. Frozen shoulder: refer and follow the physio.
Progress by: Bigger range, narrower grip on pass throughs
Regress by: Smaller range, table slides
Default prescription: 1 to 2 sets x 5 to 10 slow reps (or 30 to 60s), in warm up or cool down | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID, BURN
Sources: Squat University, DeFranco (Simple 6), Rehab practice

FAMILY iso_shoulder | Shoulder: isometrics
Muscles: Rotator cuff, deltoid
Why: Pushing into a wall or band without moving loads the shoulder tendons with almost no irritation. First line for painful rotator cuffs.
Cues: 1) Elbow at the side bent to 90 degrees 2) Push into the wall/band gently and build to about 70% 3) Hold 30 to 45s, breathe
Faults: Shrugging; Pushing to pain; Holding the breath
Cautions: Rehab friendly.
Progress by: Longer holds, harder push, move to band rotations
Regress by: Lighter push
Default prescription: 3 to 5 x 20 to 45s holds, rest 60s, about 70% effort | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Rehab research, Squat University

FAMILY raise | Shoulder: raises (isolation)
Muscles: Side and front shoulders
Why: Isolation work for shoulder muscle and shape. Light, controlled, high rep.
Cues: 1) Slight elbow bend, raise to shoulder height 2) Lead with the elbows, thumbs slightly up 3) Lower slowly
Faults: Swinging; Shrugging; Going heavy
Cautions: Keep below shoulder height with impingement.
Progress by: Cable, slower, leaning variation
Regress by: Lighter, band
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG
Sources: Poliquin, Schoenfeld

FAMILY rotator | Shoulder: rotator cuff
Muscles: Rotator cuff (infraspinatus, teres minor, subscapularis)
Why: The rotator cuff keeps the ball centred in the socket. Strong external rotators protect the shoulder during pressing (Poliquin's structural balance). Light, frequent and precise.
Cues: 1) Elbow at the side (towel under the arm) or at 90 degrees 2) Rotate the forearm out slowly 3) Return with control
Faults: Elbow drifting away; Rotating the body; Going heavy
Cautions: Shoulder rehab friendly. 90/90 versions are harder: progress there.
Progress by: Side lying DB → cable → 90/90
Regress by: Band light, isometric
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 12 to 20, slow | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Poliquin, Cressey, Squat University

FAMILY scap | Shoulder: scapular control
Muscles: Mid and lower traps, rhomboids, lats
Why: Training the shoulder blade to move (down, back, up) before the arm pulls. Scapula pull ups and retractions are the first step towards rows and pull ups (G1 of the Optimal row screen).
Cues: 1) Arms straight 2) Move only the shoulder blades 3) Pause and return
Faults: Bending the elbows; Shrugging; Rushing
Cautions: Rehab friendly.
Progress by: Full rows/pull ups
Regress by: Smaller range
Default prescription: 2 to 3 sets x 10 to 15 reps, tempo 2-1-1-1, rest 45 to 60s, RPE 8 | Sessions: STRONG, HYBRID
Sources: Squat University, Cressey

FAMILY wall_slide | Shoulder: scapular control (wall slides)
Muscles: Lower traps, serratus, T spine extension
Why: Sliding the arms up the wall trains the shoulder blade to rotate upward properly, which is key for pain free overhead movement. Part of the Simple 6 style warm up.
Cues: 1) Back or forearms against the wall, ribs down 2) Slide the arms up keeping contact, shoulders down 3) Slide back down, squeezing the shoulder blades
